    in Texas who said he opened
    fire because he was trying to
    defend fellow Muslims.
       According to testimony,
    Hasan walked into a crowded
    building at Fort Hood on Nov.
    5, 2009, with 420 rounds of
    ammunition stuffed in his
    pockets and paper towels to
    muffle the sound of the bul-
    lets. He yelled “Allahu ak-
    bar”—Arabic for “God is
    great”—and methodically shot
    at soldiers, repeatedly shoot-
    ing wounded soldiers as they
    tried to escape.
       Hasan was eventually shot
    and paralyzed from the waist
    down in the attack. He killed 13
    people and wounded 32 more.
       Hasan admitted during his
    court-martial that he carried
    out the shooting because he
    believed the military was at
    war with Islam. During the
    trial, he acted as his own law-
    yer and offered no defense for
    the attack. He was sentenced
    to death in 2013 by a jury of
    fellow officers.
       Hasan has since been held
    on death row at the military
    prison in Fort Leavenworth,
    Kan. The Army, which is re-
    quired to provide him legal
    counsel, declined to comment
    on his behalf. It would be the
    first execution of a service-
    member in 65 years.
       Only a president can ap-
    prove the execution of U.S.
    servicemembers, who are sub-
    ject to the military’s own legal
    system. Trump approved
    Hasan’s firing squad execution
    on Oct. 2 upon Defense Secre-
    tary Pete Hegseth’s recom-
    mendation, the Pentagon said.
       Army regulation says the
    execution must take place no



    Pike Out
    Of Hospital
    Following
    Botched
    Execution
    BY JOSEPH PISANI

       Christa Pike, the Tennessee
    inmate who survived the
    state’s attempt to execute her
    by lethal injection, was dis-
    charged from the hospital and
    is back in prison, her attor-
    neys said.
       Pike, who is 50, returned
    Saturday to Debra K. Johnson
    Rehabilitation Center in Nash-
    ville, where she has been for
    more than 30 years.
       “She is continuing to re-
    ceive medical care at the
    prison, and we will continue
    to track her recovery,” Pike’s
    attorneys said.
       Pike, who was sentenced to
    death in 1996 for the murder
    of Colleen Slemmer, was
    rushed to a local hospital fol-
    lowing the botched execution
    Sept. 30. Pike received two
    doses of pentobarbital, a seda-
    tive that at least 17 states use
    for capital punishment, but
    the execution was called off
    after she was still alive follow-
    ing the two injections.
       The Tennessee Department
    of Correction said it followed
    established protocol and that
    the drug has been effective.
    Tennessee Gov. Bill Lee, a Re-
    publican, has since halted exe-
    cutions in the state.
       At the hospital, Pike was at
    first intubated and put on a
    ventilator as hospital staff
    worked to clear the pentobar-
    bital from her system. In re-
    cent days, Pike’s attorneys
    said she was conscious and
    speaking.
       Her legal team has called
    on the governor to commute
    her death sentence and allow
    her to live the rest of her life
    in prison.

                                                                                                                     If children are the future, their now matters.
                                                                                                                      Their health matters now. Their education and their path
                                                                                                                      toward a livelihood matter now. Above all, their safety
                                                                                                                      matters now.                                                                            Because we need each other.

                                                                                                                      ChildFund has always brought together people who care
                                                                                                                      about children to help them grow up healthy, educated,
         8=?*(," &=A'?(,,:" *?8 *8&=7*(,>
                                                                                                                      skilled and safe. And that matters for all of us.                                           ChildFund.org

                                                                                                                                                                                                                this level, interns aren’t fetching

                                                                                                                                                                                                                around $300,000.

                                                                                                                                                                                                                the unofficial motto of students

                                                                                                                                                                                                                Capital intern told me. The think-

                                                                                                                                                                                                                only lied about their ages to get
                                                                                                                                                                                                                into bars.

                                                                                                                                                                                                                desperation. A lot of high-achiev-
                                                                                                                                                                                                                ing students have an unhealthy
                                                                                                                                                                                                                fear of falling behind, says Joe Ca-
                                                                                                                                                                                                                trino, executive director of the
                                                                                                                                                                                                                Dartmouth Center for Career De-

                    A14 | Monday, October 12, 2026                                                                                                                            THE WALL STREET JOURNAL.


                                                                                      ARTS IN REVIEW
                    BY LAURA JACOBS




                T
                                               New York
                             he story for Paramount’s
                             1927 silent movie “It” was
                             provided by the British
                             writer Elinor Glyn, who
                             used the word to mean a
                    “magnetic force” that draws oth-
                    ers. The 21-year-old actress Clara
                    Bow, who starred in the film, was
                    henceforth known as the first “It
                    Girl.” Charisma? Glamour? Not
                    quite. Her creamy cheeks, tousled
                    bob and bright, beguiling eyes
                    brought a hint of id, jolts of en-
                    ergy, into the picture. And energy
                    was what moving pictures were all
                    about. Where photographs were
                    still, and theater could talk, silent
                    movies were action and “It” was
                    everything.
                        That the mute infant art form
                    soon relied on fashions that ex-
                    cited the eye should come as no
                    surprise. Glyn’s older sister, Lady
                    Duff-Gordon, otherwise known as
                    the couturier Lucile, immediately
                    forged relationships with ac-
                    tresses—Billie Burke, for instance.
                    As early as 1913 she was design-
                    ing heart-stopping gowns for
                    their movies, which in turn
                    brought renown to her fashion
                    house. What is surprising is that
                    “Goddesses in the Machine: Fash-
                    ion in American Silent Film,” at
                    New York’s Bard Graduate Center,
                    is the first major exhibition to ex-
                    plore the subject.
                        Launched in the U.S. in 1896,
                    with the New York premiere of
                    Thomas Edison’s Vitascope film
                    projector, silent movies aston-
                    ished audiences. And yet the me-
                    dium, entirely visual, posed defi-
                    nite challenges. “Seeking ways to
                    craft narratives without audible
                    dialogue,” reads the show’s intro-      Installation view of ‘Goddesses in the Machine,’ above; matador jacket worn by Rudolph Valentino, below.
                    ductory wall text, “filmmakers
                    used costume to convey character,
                    drive plot, and heighten emotion.”      EXHIBITION REVIEW


                                                            Lights, Camera, Costumes!
                    The first costumes were made by
                    dressmakers and couturiers, but a
                    network of costume professionals
                    would emerge, designers who re-
                    alized that clothing in film had to
                    be fascinating, energized—stories
                    within the story.                       As silent movies emerged as an art form, fashion played an essential, energizing role on screen
                        The show’s co-organizers—Mi-
                    chelle Finamore, a fashion and de-      Travis Banton evening                                                                  the faces of stars         immaculate canary-yellow dress,
